### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 澳洲央行決議如預期升息一碼至 4.60%為今年第四度升息，基準利率創 2011 年
以來新高，澳洲央行在政策聲明中表示通膨壓力尚未緩解，不排除在11、12 月
政策會議進一步升息；另一方面，澳洲8 月消費者物價指數(CPI)月增 0.4%略低
於預期，但年增 4.0%仍高於澳洲央行 2~3%的目標區間。
⚫ 美國第二季美 GDP 終值從 1.5%上修至 2.2%， 其中實質個人消費支出創下2025
年 3 月以來最大單月增幅，顯示即使高利率持續，美國家庭消費依然具韌性；
另一方面，美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增月增 0.2%、3.0%，雙雙低於市場預
期的 0.3%、3.3%，美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬人低於預期的 9 萬人，失
業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，8 月非農就業人數亦同步下修至 13.3 萬人，通膨下滑
和勞動力市場略有降溫，降低市場對聯準會 10 月升息預期，Fedwatch 顯示 10

月升息機率下降至 20%左右。
⚫ 受美伊衝突引發的能源價格衝擊影響 ，9 月歐元地區消費者物價調和指數(HICP)
整體上升速度遠超預期，其中，法國年增 3.4%、德國年增 3.3%，歐元區年增
3.8%，均高於歐洲央行的 2%目標，反映能源價格的衝擊持續影響物價，加深歐
洲央行再次升息的壓力。
⚫ 日本上週公佈貨幣政策會議紀要及央行意見摘要顯示多名委員認為當前政策利
率依然處於中性區間之下 ，需警惕通膨上行風險 ， 且未來應該以更快速度升息抑
制通膨，然而意見摘要提及日本經濟財政大臣城內實表示希望央行充分審視過
去升息對景氣之影響 ，市場解讀為政府牽制日本央行未來升息步伐 ， 而財務大臣
片山皋月則重申尊重日本央行的自主性。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Oct/5/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.760，低點在 31.946，以 31.830 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 5 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶，週初，國際油價持穩於每桶 100 美元上方，市場預期聯準
會後續仍有升息的空間，提振美元指數表現，加以外資賣超台股並匯出，台幣走貶
至 31.900 上方；不過適逢月底也是季底，匯價逢高吸引出口商進場賣美金，緩解台
幣貶值的速度；臨近週末，美國 8 月核心 PCE 數據 3%低於預期 3.3%，但市場等待
9 月非農就業數據，交投偏向謹慎觀望，最終在央行調節之下收在 31.830。

＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬
人，低於市場預期 9 萬人，且失業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，顯示就業市場動能略有
減弱，市場對於 10 月升息的預期下降，但仍預期 12 月會再度升息。美元指數雖然
脫離高點，但仍在 102 附近高位震盪，預計將給台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，
台股再創收盤新高且外資轉為買超台股，且隨著 AI 需求持續帶動台灣經濟出口表
現強勁，預計出口商後續拋匯需求將持續提供台幣支撐。綜合以上，預估本週台幣
區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Oct/5/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪偏升。隨著美中兩國完成新一輪談判，雙方同意延長休兵，從
原定的 11 月延長到明年 1 月 ， 降低短期貿易摩擦升溫的風險 ， 支撐人民幣的走勢由
6.7170 走升至 6.7030；不過由於美伊戰事持續 ，國際油價持穩於每桶 100 美元上方 ，
加以美元指數及美債收益率雙雙走高 ， 令人民幣由升轉貶至6.7200 上方 ； 臨近週末，
美國公布 9 月非農數據降溫，聯準會10 月升息預期下滑，美元指數回落，境外人民
幣走升至 6.7000 週內高點，終場境外人民幣收在 6.7061，週線升值 166 點，境內人
民幣於十一長假前夕收盤於在 6.7053，週線升值 72 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週人民幣區間震盪 。 就國際市場來看 ， 美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬
人，遠低於市場預期 9 萬人，且失業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，顯示就業市場動能略
為減弱。數據公布後，市場對於聯準會10 月升息的預期下降，美元指數小幅回落，
有助於人民幣的表現；就中國國內市場來看，人行中間價格再度升破 6.7400，有助
於人民幣的走勢 ， 不過10/1~10/7 是中國十一長假 ， 預計人民幣走勢主要將受國際美
元影響；最後就技術面來看，USD/CNH 日線圖中，各天期均線仍向下發散，空頭格
局不變，不過匯價反彈至 20 日均線附近，顯示下行動能暫緩。綜合以上，預估本週
人民幣區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6900-6.7300

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Oct/5/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY 震盪上行。週初，美元指數持穩在 101 以上高位，USD/JPY 大致
在 156.50 至 157.85 間波動；週間，適逢日本上半年會計年度結算，出口商拋匯使資
金匯回力道強勁 ， 帶動USD/JPY 下行最低來到 156.35，但逢低有買盤進場，USD/JPY
回到 157 整數關卡之上，後隨美債殖利率創高、美元指數突破 102 關卡，USD/JPY
上行最高來至 158.45；臨近週末，美國公布 9 月非農就業人數低於預期，USD/JPY
一度回落至 157 下方，終場收在 157.83，週線上漲 57 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪 ， 主要原因如下 ： 一 、日本 9 月製造業 PMI 由 54.9
回落至 54.1，增長速度放慢至六個月以來的低位，主因為產出和新訂單增長放緩，
經濟復甦力道可能減緩，不利日圓表現。二、美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增 3.0%，
美國 9 月非農數據 2.9 萬人均低於預期，通膨和勞動力市場略有降溫使市場對聯準
會升息預期下滑，加之部分聯準會官員表示無需急於進一步升息，Fedwatch 顯示 10
月升息機率降至兩成，然而美國第二季 GDP 上修至 2.2%經濟基本面穩健仍將支撐
美元。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價處於區間波動，且各天期均線
趨平，上方 158.50 有年線、季線壓力，下方 156.25 則有月線支撐，預期短線以區間
震盪可能性較高。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：155.50-159.00 
前週交易區間 
156.35 158.45

### Page 6

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Oct/5/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，美元指數持穩在 101 以上高位，EUR/USD 震盪下
行至 1.1310，而在紐約聯儲主席威廉姆斯發表不急於再次升息的偏鴿言論後，
EUR/USD 反彈至 1.1340；週間，美國 8 月 PCE 數據低於預期，EUR/USD 一度反彈
至 1.1380，然而在全球債券拋售潮下，資金回流美元帶動美元指數突破 102 關卡，
EUR/USD 跌破 1.1300 關卡最低來至 1.1214；臨近週末，美國 9 月非農就業人數低
於預期，EUR/USD 反彈至 1.1285 後回落，終場收在 1.1253，週線下跌 138 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、上週公佈歐元地區消費者
物價調和指數(HICP)，法國年增 3.4%、德國年增 3.3%、歐元區年增 3.8%，顯示能
源價格衝擊依然使通膨壓力居高不下， 市場預期歐洲央行可能在 12 月再度升息 ， 將
帶給歐元部分支撐。二、美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增 3.0%、月增 0.2%低於預
期，而 9 月非農就業人數 2.9 萬人低於預期的 9 萬人 ， 失業率則由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，
就業降溫及通膨回落降低市場對聯準會十月升息的預期，然而第二季美國 GDP 終
值從 1.5%上修至 2.2%，經濟基本面持穩仍有利美元維持強勢。三、技術面而言，在
日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價在各天期均線下方並跌破 1.1300 整數關卡，月線向下穿
過季線形成死亡交叉，顯示短線空方動能較強。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈
現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1150-1.1400 
前週交易區間 
1.1214 1.1391

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                           新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.29-115.10.02 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.65% ~ 1.68% 
公債－10 年期-A15109     1.95% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.82%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ， 本週為提存期底 ， 繼央行上週大砍存單效應持續發酵 ， 市
場資金供給仍充裕，惟需求疲弱，帶動短率朝區間低位移動。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.66%~1.68%，利率高位較上週下跌 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週市場交投仍冷清，5 年期指標券 A15108 週三於處所成交在 1.82%，10 年
期指標券則未成交，週五等殖系統參考報價中價為 1.95%。 
第四季台債發行量雖偏少 ， 惟美伊和談依舊陷入僵局 ， 國際油價續處高位 ， 通
膨疑慮尚存，國際債市走勢仍偏空，預期台債殖利率短線將維持高檔盤整格
局，預估 5 年券區間為 1.85%~1.90%，10 年券區間為 1.95%~2%。

鴻海 (2317 TT) 
上修獲利效益預期

永豐觀點 
垂直整合能力，維持 AI 伺服器領先地位，營收規模擴大，上修獲利效益預期。 
 投資評價與建議 
研究部維持鴻海買進建議：鴻海集團於科技日展現垂直整合、精密加工能力，並憑
藉著規模化交付能力， 預期於伺服器市場維持四成以上市占率 、並能優先取得新世
代產品參考設計， 營收規模放大的同時體現獲利率效益， 獲利金額擴大以及營運資
金運用，並樹立組裝產業進入門檻。研究部參考近期公告之財務及產業資訊，預期
鴻海 2H26~2027 年營運具上修空間 ， 調整後預估鴻海 2026 年/2027 年稅後 EPS 
18.79 元/25.46 元。惟近期總體利率環境影響股價評價，目標本益比從 15 倍調整
至 14 倍，維持鴻海買進建議，目標價 356 元(14x2027FEPS)。

ESG 評析 
鴻海企業永續評鑑整體為 A 等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 A 等與 A 等。 
SinoPac+ ESG 評鑑系統評等

資料來源：SinoPac+ ESG 評等 (依循 2025 SinoPac+企業永續評鑑方法學) 
註 1：資誠永續發展服務股份有限公司僅於ＥＳＧ評鑑系統方法學建置過程中，就評分指標提供
專業意見，對於評分結果及評估報告內容之完整性及真實性，不負擔保責任，亦不對閱讀或使用
本評估報告之第三方負任何責任。

### Page 2

2

鴻海為專業 ODM/EMS 廠商，產品涵蓋手機、伺服器、平板、網通設備等終端產
品，以及各類電子零組件如連接器、連接線等，旗下重要子公司包含工業富聯
(601138 SH)、富智康(2038 HK)及鴻騰精密(6088 HK)，近年公司開始積極投入研
發資源在電動車 、 數位醫療 、 機器人 、AI、 半導體 、5G 等領域 。 主要客戶為Apple、
HP、Dell、Sony、Cisco、北美四大 CSP 等。2Q26 產品營收占比：消費暨智能
29%、雲端網路 51%、電腦終端 15%、元件及其他 5%。 
鴻海科技日展現垂直整合能力  統合零組件到機櫃生產：(1) 鴻海科技日強調展現
垂直整合能力及精密加工能力，提供客戶 one stop shop，垂直整合、設計製造服
務。鴻海具備伺服器 Level 1 到 Level 12 的生產能力，從螺絲、零組件、機構件
等至伺服器系統整機櫃。鴻海團隊從產品設計開始即整合組裝、良率、服務零組件
到整系統最佳化的解決方案。(2) 鴻海協助輝達、Google 打造 AI 工廠、AI 基礎
建設，以自動化縮短資料中心建造時程。(3) 施耐德電機、東元提供電力設備，與
鴻海進一步合作打造模組化資料中心。 4. 本田與三菱電機展示與鴻海在電動車未
來自動駕駛、駕駛監控系統、 ECU 等合作方向。 (5) 鴻海同時推出新電動車平
台,Model A+ 、K 系列。A+為休旅車，其特色為取消 B 柱讓車內寬敞，並將配置 
L4 自動駕駛感測器。Model K 為輕型卡車，提供短程 120 公里、長程 250 公里車
型選擇。 
展場上鴻海集團展出 AI 伺服器相關產品，1. 與 Vertiv 合作液冷基建的 AI Data 
Hall 解決方案。(2) 展出 NVIDIA DSX POD 解決方案，以及 Vera Rubin 世代 
Compute Tray、Switch Tray、CPO Switch、CPU Tray、LPU Tray 等相關產品。
(3) Google 下一代 TPU 將使用的 OCS 全光交換機。(4) 在 NVIDIA CPO Switch
使用 的精 密 光學 對位 機 構 件、FAU 光纖 連 接產 品。(5) 集團 公司 鴻 騰精密
(6088.HK)， 展出液冷相關零組件QD、Manifold； 電力相關零組件Busbar、Power 
Whip；主板上連接器 Paladin、SOCAMM 等。甚至下一代 Vera Rubin Ultra 將
採用 ZQD、POGO 連接器也已取得 NPI。 
美系大廠資本支出 2027 年上修至 6 成以上增長 建置需求未歇：上週美股法說會
後，統計 Bloomberg 美系五大 CSP 加上 Space X，2026 年資本支出從年增率從
分別從 102%上修至 103%，2027 年從 44%上修至 60%，2026 年及 2027 年資
本支出金額分別達 8,793 億美元及 1.41 兆美元，顯示AI 算力建置需求仍未停歇。 
上修 3Q26、4Q26 營收獲利預估：鴻海先前公告 3Q26 營收達 3.0 兆優於預期，
以及子公司工業富聯公告 3Q26 財報優於預期。以此推算鴻海營收擴大規模效益
帶來獲利表現上修，研究部預期營益率增長至 3.8%，稅後 EPS 上修至 5.10 元。
上修 4Q26 營收預期，主因3Q26 營運基期墊高，以及iPhone 維持出貨動能、GB
機櫃出貨量出貨量、Vera Rubin 機櫃單價跳升等，推算帶動營收季增 14%，稅後
EPS 上修至 5.85 元。

營運現況與分析

### Page 3

3

AI 伺服器需求為鴻海營運增長動能：AI 伺服器需求主要由美系 CSP、AI 模型商及
Neocloud 驅動，鴻海 GPU AI 伺服器除了原本美系 CSP 需求，也將有新型雲端
客戶開始貢獻。研究部預期鴻海維持 VR200 的主要供應商角色，整體市占率維持
達 40%以上，近期上修預估整體市場 Vera Rubin 至約 64,000 櫃 NVL72 (市場預
期為 80,000~90,000 櫃)，同時鴻海 2027 年將增加美系 CSP AI ASIC 伺服器機櫃
供應，為 2027 年營運增長動能，鴻海在提高市占率、營收規模放大的同時體現獲
利率效益、獲利金額擴大以及營運資金運用，並能樹立組裝產業進入門檻。 
綜合以上，上修後預估 2026 年營收 11.1 兆，年增 37%，毛利率 6.0%，營益率
3.7%，稅後淨利 2,627 億，年增 39%，稅後 EPS 18.79 元。2027 年營收 15.5 兆
元，年增 39%，毛利率 5.4%，營益率 3.4%，稅後淨利 3,555 億元，年增 35%，
稅後 EPS 25.46 元。

Bull/Bear: Globalwafers Co (6488.TWO)
Prepared for Kevin Lu

### Page 6

Globalwafers Co (6488.TWO)
04 November 2026 Citi Research
6
Globalwafers Co
Company description
GWC is the world's third-largest silicon wafer manufacturer. The company
offers a one-stop shop for customers with its wide range of product portfolio
and global presence. GWC has 16 manufacturing sites in 10 countries spread
across Asia, Europe, and the Americas. Its product offering include annealed,
polished, non-polished, diffused, FZ, EPI, SOI wafers ranging from 4” to 12” in

diameter.
Investment strategy
We rate GWC as Buy as we expect its sales and earnings to recover in 2027.
We believe silicon wafer sector has hit the bottom in 2Q26, evidenced by UTR
improvements across 6” to 12” wafers. Although GM would likely face short-
term headwinds in 2H26 due to rising depreciation burden, a fire incident and
higher energy costs, we believe favorable pricing trend, full UTR and the
reception of US government’s subsidies would help offset the negative

impact, especially in 2027.
Valuation
Our target price of NT$920 is based on 28x 2H27-1H28E EPS, in line with
+1stdv of 1-year fwd PE in the past 5 years. We use this PE multiple to reflect a
gradually improving demand outlook for the overall semiconductor industry
from 2H26 onward, especially a more meaningful recovery in 2027 and likely
shortage. We believe the upcoming megatrend demand from AI, HPC, auto,
and Io T and supply tightness could justify GWC's valuations during recovery

backdrop from 1Q27 onwards.
Risks
Key downside risks that could cause the GWC shares to trade below our
target price include: 1) weaker-than-expected pull-in demand; 2)
undisciplined capacity expansion by other industry participants; 3) wafer
price decline driven by macro factors; and 4) late reception of the subsidies
from the US government.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
What's new? Earlier today, a fire broke out at Panasonic's (covered by Kazuo
Yoshikawa) CCL factory in Hsinchu, Taiwan. The fire was extinguished before noon
(see Reuters' report), but Panasonic has yet to issue an official statement in
response to the incident.
Our view: Our checks indicate that this plant has monthly capacity of ~200K sheets

and primarily produces M6/7 and some lower-end BT substrate CCLs. Panasonic also
operates CCL sites in Fukushima, Japan; Guangzhou and Suzhou, China; and
Ayutthaya, Thailand, which remains under construction. Most high-end M7/8 CCL is
produced in Fukushima, while the China sites supply much of its mainstream CCL.
Our supply chain checks suggest Panasonic is reallocating capacity toward high-end
CCL for AI applications – particularly a key US hyperscaler’s in-house ASICs, as high-

end CCL supply remains tight. Although the Hsinchu plant is immaterial to group
capacity, the fire could accelerate this shift, potentially driving near-term customer
order overflow to Taiwanese CCL competitors.
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with
companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result,
investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of
interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley
Research. Investors should consider Morgan Stanley

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.
Key Takeaways
A fire at Panasonic's CCL factory in Hsinchu may push near-term orderflow to
Taiwanese competitors.
Within our coverage, we see EMC as most likely to receive orders, followed by
TUC and Shengyi Tech (EW).
EMC is also a high-end CCL supplier to Panasonic’s key ASIC customer.
Valuations are attractive for EMC and TUC, and we remain OW on both.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.

Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

- 1 - 
個股報告 
環球晶(6488) 
Novara 火災延後獲利回升，2027調價及新廠效益顯現 
投資評等 目標價 
逢低買進 $810 
前次投資建議：2026.08.05 
「逢低買進」 ，目標價: $1160

報告日期：2026.11.04 研 究 員：李彝安

摘 要 及 調 整 原 因 
➢ 26Q3 獲利/毛利不如預期。諾瓦拉廠火災延後營運回升時程，下修 2026 年、2027 年 EPS 
至  18.7 元、 27 元，價格調升與新產能自  27Q1 起反映，目標價下修至  810 元
(30x2027(F)PER)，維持「逢低買進」評等

基本資料 結論與建議 
2026/11/03 收盤價： 685.0 ➢ 環球晶 26Q3 合併營收 148.7 億元（QoQ -2.3%／
YoY +2.6%）低於研究部原先預估，主營收季減主要受
7月義大利 Novara 8 吋廠火災影響，除部分出貨遞延
外，亦造成跨廠產能調配及產品組合變動，使毛利率承
壓單季 EPS 3.13 元，低於 26Q2 之 7.90 元，除火
災影響外，26Q2 認列之世創（Siltronic）評價利益本
季未再發生。 
➢ 需求面未受火災影響。排除爬坡中之新產線，既有 12 
吋產能滿載，8 吋與 6 吋接近滿載，26Q4 需求高於
原先預期，部分客戶為取得產能願將調價提前至 
26Q4。諾瓦拉後段製程仍在重建，支援廠區本身接近
滿載，26Q4 復原量有限。研究部下修 2026 年營收至 
596.4 億元、毛利率至 20.6%，2026 年 EPS 自 21.7 
元下修至 18.7 元。 
➢ 2027 年價格調升自 27Q1 起反映，晶圓庫存較高之客
戶對調價接受度較低，整體 ASP 調升將分散於各季反
映；新廠折舊於未來仍將持續增加。研究部就調價幅度
與新廠成本吸收速度採較保守假設，2027 年營收自 
836.2 億元下修至 762 億元、毛利率自 29.5%下修至 
26.6%，2027 年 EPS 自 34.1 元下修至 27 元。 
➢ 考量 2027 年調價幅度因客戶而異、新廠折舊仍在增
加，合併毛利率回升速度慢於前次預估；目標價自 
1,160 元下修至 810 元(30x2027(F)PER)，潛在上漲
空間 18.2%，評等維持「逢低買進」。

資料來源：CMoney；兆豐投顧預估

營 運 分 析 
全球前三大矽晶圓供應商，布局高階與第三代半導體材料 
環球晶圓股份有限公司（6488）成立於 2011 年，由中美矽晶半導體事業分
割成立，總部位於新竹科學園區，為全球前三大、台灣最大矽晶圓供應商，
亦為全球最大非日系矽晶圓廠。公司產品涵蓋  3 至 12 吋拋光、磊晶與  
Silicon-on-Insulator（SOI）晶圓，應用橫跨邏輯 IC、記憶體、功率元件、
車用、工控與射頻通訊等領域，主要客戶包括整合元件製造商（ Integrated 
Device Manufacturer, IDM） 與晶圓代工廠，競爭對手為信越化學、SUMCO、
Siltronic 與 SK Siltron。 
近年公司資本支出重心由既有亞洲據點延伸至美國與歐洲，推動 Texas 12 
吋矽晶圓新廠、Missouri SOI 擴產及義大利相關專案，以支應 AI、高效能
運算（High Performance Computing, HPC）、先進記憶體、矽光與在地化
供應鏈需求。美國商務部已核定對環球晶  Texas 與 Missouri 專案提供  
4.06 億美元補助，其中  Texas 廠將建立美國本土  300mm 矽晶圓量產能
力，Missouri 則聚焦 SOI 晶圓擴產。 
除傳統矽晶圓外，公司亦布局碳化矽（Silicon Carbide, SiC）、特殊晶圓與
方形矽晶圓等高附加價值材料，對應第三代半導體與先進封裝需求。

- 3 - 
個股報告

諾瓦拉廠火災壓低 26Q3 營收，營業利益仍較 26Q2 成長 
環球晶 26Q3 營收 148.71 億元（QoQ -2.3%／YoY +2.6%） ，低於研究部原
估 161.88 億元，主要受7月義大利 Novara 廠 8吋產線火災影響。該廠除供
應 8吋晶圓外，亦為集團 SOI產品提供基板，火災造成部分製程停工，其他
廠區須調整產能支援，使整體出貨受到影響。公司表示，若未發生火災，26Q3
營收原有機會季增逾 12%，顯示營收下滑主要來自供給受限，而非客戶需求
轉弱。 
26Q3 毛利率 20.0%（QoQ -0.6ppt／YoY +1.6ppt） ，主要受新產能爬坡成本、
折舊增加及跨廠調配造成產品組合變動影響。營業利益 14.54 億元（QoQ 
+2.2%） ，營益率9.8%（QoQ +0.4ppt） ，部分受惠Siltronic股價變動使相關
酬勞提列減少 。 稅後淨利15.05 億元 （QoQ -60.2%／YoY -23.6%），EPS 3.13
元，較26Q2 的 7.90 元明顯下滑，主因Siltronic股價於 26Q2 上漲約 56%，
公司認列較高評價利益，26Q3 股價變動不大，業外貢獻因而減少。

Novara後段製程仍待修復，26Q4營收恢復季增 
Novara 8 吋廠主要製程已陸續恢復，磊晶產品亦開始出貨，惟部分後段製程
仍在重建，暫由集團其他廠區支援。由於支援廠區稼動率已高，且各廠產品
規格及客戶認證要求不同，可轉移產能有限，短期出貨仍難恢復至火災前水
準。部分關鍵設備交期較長，後段製程完全復原時程尚未確定；相關財產損
失及營業中斷保險理賠已啟動，惟金額尚未確認，公司尚未認列理賠收益。
至於 Novara 12 吋新廠位於獨立廠房，未受火災影響，目前已開始量產出貨，
擴產進度持續推進。 
26Q4 客戶需求優於原先預期，部分客戶為確保產能供應，願提前接受新價
格，惟多數訂單仍適用原有長約或2025 年議定價格，加上Novara 產能復原
有限 ， 預期26Q4 營收將優於 26Q3， 但季增幅度低於12%。 研究部預估26Q4
營收 155.78 億元（ QoQ +4.8% ／YoY +7.4% ） ，毛利率21.0%（QoQ 
+1.0ppt） ，稅後淨利17.54 億元，EPS 3.67元。由於 26Q4 實際調價幅度有
限，短期營收改善仍以既有產能出貨及 Novara 逐步復原為主。

既有產能接近滿載，2027年新長約帶動 ASP改善 
排除尚在爬坡的新產線 ， 環球晶既有12吋產能已滿載 ，8吋及 6吋接近滿載，
部分小尺寸晶圓需求亦超過現有供給。隨半導體廠陸續擴充先進製程與記憶
體產能，晶圓投片需求有望增加，惟新產能仍須經過設備安裝及客戶認證，
具備量產資格的 12吋晶圓供給短期難以快速增加。公司預期2027 年矽晶圓
供需將較 2026 年緊俏，為新一輪價格調整提供支撐。 
價格方面，能源、原物料、人力及運輸成本上升，加上新廠建置成本提高，
環球晶正與客戶協商新長約價格。由於既有長約仍須履行，且不同客戶庫存
及需求狀況不一，調價將依產品規格、供需及換約時點逐案進行，並非採取

- 4 - 
個股報告 
固定漲幅。部分庫存較高的客戶對價格調整接受度較低，公司會優先將產能
分配予願意接受新價格、簽訂長約或支付預付款的客戶。研究部預期隨2027
年新長約陸續生效、部分現貨報價改善及高階產品占比提升，整體ASP將逐
季回升。 
12吋 SOI需求超過既有擴產規劃，矽光子提供後續成長動能 
環球晶 12吋 SOI 需求受惠製程由 8吋轉向 12 吋，以及矽光子、RF-SOI及
Power SOI應用成長，客戶需求已超過密蘇里廠既有擴產規劃。公司目前優
先透過設備認證及製程去瓶頸提高現有產能，部分 RF-SOI 及矽光子產品已
進入量產。由於密蘇里廠現有廠房及基礎設施已就位，下一階段擴產主要以
增購設備為主，資本效率可望優於第一階段。 
公司正與多家客戶洽談長期供貨協議及預付款安排，規劃中的客戶承諾可支
持擬新增 SOI產能逾九成需求，惟相關合約及擴產決策仍未完全確定。後續
投資時程將取決於現有產能獲利改善程度、客戶長期承諾及政府補助條件，
研究部暫不將尚未核定的新增產能全數納入 2027 年營收預估。此外，SiC
需求逐步由電動車延伸至資料中心、電網及工業應用，12吋 SiC仍處初期爬
坡階段，預期 2027 年營收貢獻仍低，對短期獲利影響有限。

營業利益 60.34 億元，稅後淨利 89.30 億元（YoY +22.1%），EPS 18.68
元；2027 年營收 762.03 億元（YoY +27.8%），毛利率 26.6%，營業利益
135.76 億元（YoY +125.0%），稅後淨利 129.04億元（YoY +44.5%），
EPS 26.99元。

- 5 - 
個股報告 
本次下修 2026 年 EPS 由 21.69 元至 18.68 元，主要反映 26Q3 Novara 火
災影響大於原先預期，以及26Q4 出貨恢復與毛利率改善幅度有限；2027年
EPS 則由 34.12 元下修至 26.99 元，主要反映營收由 836 億元降至 762 億
元，毛利率由 29.5%下修至 26.6%。考量 2027 年新長約價格逐步調整、
Novara 產能復原，以及美國、義大利與日本新廠出貨增加，研究部預期本業
獲利仍將逐季回升，惟新產能折舊與價格協商進度將拖累獲利改善幅度。

從結論來看，環球晶 26Q3 受 Novara 火災影響，延後原先預期的營收與毛
利率回升時程，不過既有產線稼動率仍維持高檔，2027 年調價及海外新廠出
貨增加的成長方向未變。考量 2027 年矽晶圓價格調整幅度及新廠稼動率提
升速度仍具不確定性，研究部將評價倍數由34 倍下修至 30倍 2027F PER。
以 2027 年預估 EPS 26.99 元計算，目標價由 1,160 元調降至 810 元，潛在
上漲空間 18.2%，維持「逢低買進」評等。

SK하이닉스 (000660)

Company Brief 
반도체/디스플레이 
Analyst 김선우 
sunwoo.kim@meritz.co.kr 
RA 우서현 
seohyun.woo@meritz.co.kr 3Q26 OP 80.1조원 실적 서프라이즈 예상  
3Q26 영업이익: 상반기와 다르다. 억제 요인들의 추세 반전 전환 
SK하이닉스의 3Q26 영업이익은 전분기 대비 32% 증가한 80.1조원으로 시장기대
치를 능가할 전망이다. 최근 원화강세 흐름 및 상반기 동사의 상대적 실적개선 속도 
열위에 3Q26 영업이익 추정치는 70조원까지 내려갔다 (컨센서스 74조원). 하지만 
이제 동사 출하량 및 판가의 탄력적 복원세가 경쟁사 보다 빠른 실적 개선을 이끌 전
망이다. 3Q26 DRAM과 NAND 출하량/판가 증감률은 각각 +13%/+22%, 
+4%/+15% 수준으로, 각 영업이익은 63조원/17조원으로 예상된다 (OPM 각각 
84%/69% 추정). 그간의 상대적 실적 열위는 이제 반전된다. 이는 1) 판가 전략에 
있어 중장기적 관점에서 고객 중심 유연성 극대화를 꾀했던 부분의 보상적 되돌림이 
발생하고, 2) 업계에서 제일 높은 HBM 판매 비중으로 인해 판가 상승률에 발목 잡혔
지만, 오히려 HBM4 본격 공급이 시작되며 이익기여가 본격화된다. 아울러 3) 모바
일 핵심고객과의 기성 계약이 산업 전반적 LTA 논의로 재검토되며 공급사에 크게 유
리한 조건으로 변화되어 가기 시작한다. 당사는 1Q27부터 모바일 메모리 판가 급등
을 전망하며 (그림1), 2026E/2027E 영업이익의 경우 264조원/454조원으로 상향조정한
다. 4Q26 내 시장 실적 추정치 상향조정 및 투자자 수급의 구조적 개선을 전망한다.

‘지능기술’ 시대, 수요의 가격 역탄력성 속 중장기 기회창출에 주목 
과거 B2C 사이클과 다르게, 금번 B2B 사이클은 수요의 가격 역탄력성을 보이고 있
다. AGI 도달을 위한 선착순 투자가 핵심이기 때문이다. 빅테크 중 낙오자가 발생하
기 전까지 경쟁적 대규모 투자는 지속된다. 최근 동사의 솔리다임 상장 추진보도에 
일부 우려가 제기되고 있지만, 투자자들은 그 이면의 중장기적 기회에 주목해야 한다. 
동사는 LTA 외에도, 다양한 파트너십을 추진하며 중장기 시장 예측력을 증가시키고 
있다. 솔리다임 상장 과정에서 유입되는 현금은 모회사 ‘SK AI co’의 지분교환 및 JV 
구축 등에 활용되리라 예상된다. 이는 중장기 수요를 ‘창출’시키는 개념이다.

순수한 주주가치 제고 목적 환원 재도출 예상. 적정주가 상향조정 
빠르게 증가한 현금흐름 중 ‘잉여’분은 주주가치 ‘회복’에 우선 배분돼야 함이 마땅하
며, 동사 역시 최근 공시를 통해 주주가치 제고와 주가의 저평가 인식을 드러냈다. 현
재 진행중인 40조원 자사주 매입 프로그램 종료 후 즉각적인 신규 환원 정책 도출이 
예상된다. 2027년 메모리 공급부족은 명확하다. 역사적으로 효율적 시장 내, 주가 조
정은 탄력적 복원으로 귀결되어왔다. 동사에 대한 투자의견 Buy를 유지하며, 적정주
가의 경우 320만원으로 상향한다 (26E/27E 평균 P/B 4.0배 적용). 
 Meritz Research  2026. 10. 12

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준)

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대단기 부담_2a8cdc97.pdf

목표주가 2,000,000

1,354,000 
제약업종

◼ 3Q26 컨센서스 부합, 4Q26 성장폭 확대 전망 
◼ 5공장 Ramp-up 지속, 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목 
◼ 투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
목표주가는 DCF 방식으로 산출(WACC 8.1%, 영구성장률 3.5% 적용)

3Q26 Preview: 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 전망 
3Q26 매출액 1조 3,380억원(+6.4% yoy, +1.3% qoq), 영업이익 5,890억원(-
7.0% yoy, +0.4% qoq, OPM 44.0%)으로 컨센서스에 부합하는 실적을 전
망함. 3분기 평균 원/달러 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락하고, 지난 5월 
파업에 따른 생산 차질 영향이 반영되나, 5공장 PPQ 물량의 매출 인식이 
시작되며 yoy, qoq 외형 성장은 이어질 것으로 예상함.  
파업 당시 생산이 지연된 물량은 3Q 중 추가 생산되어 4Q26부터 매출에 반
영될 전망임. 5공장은 다수 프로젝트의 PPQ가 진행 중이며, Ramp-up에 
따라 매출 기여도 점진적으로 확대될 것으로 예상함. 이에 4Q26에는 파
업 이연 물량 반영과 5공장 매출 확대에 따라 실적 성장폭이 확대될 전
망임. 2026년 연간 매출액과 영업이익은 각각 5조 5,310억원(+21.4% 
yoy), 2조 4,780억원(+19.8% yoy, OPM 44.8%)으로 전망함.

신규 수주 회복 기대, 중장기 성장 기반은 확대 중 
관세 관련 불확실성으로 둔화됐던 고객사와의 수주 논의가 재개되고 있음. 
10월 2일 유럽 소재 제약사와 935억원 규모의 CMO 계약을 추가 체결
했으며, 4Q26 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목함.  
PolyPeptide 인수는 연내 거래 종결을 목표로 진행 중이며, 인수 완료 
시 펩타이드 분야로 CDMO 포트폴리오가 확대될 전망임. 6 공장도 연내 
착공을 목표로 준비 중임. 
한편, 노사 리스크는 단기 불확실성 요인임. 노조는 10/26부터 3주간 2
차 총파업을 예고함. 1차 대비 예정된 파업 기간이 길어진 만큼 실제 파
업 진행 시 생산 차질 규모가 주요 변수임. 다만 파업 개시 전까지 협상 
가능성이 열려 있어, 향후 노사 협상 진행 경과를 확인할 필요가 있음.

산업 투자의견 
- Overweight(비중확대)

- Neutral(중립) 
  : 향후 6개월간 업종지수상승률이 시장수익률과 유사한 수준 예상

- Buy(매수)  
  : 향후 6개월간 시장수익률 대비 10%p 이상 주가 상승 예상

  : 향후 6개월간 시장수익률 대비 -10%p~10%p 주가 변동 예상

Document 270: AI in 2027_ 20 questions for the global economy

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI in 2027_ 20 questions for the global economy.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-05
Pages: 24
Status: ok

### Page 1

The AI boom is reshaping growth, productivity, trade, jobs and
markets. Investment still has room to run in 2027, but
ﬁnancing is getting riskier and expectations are sky-high. We
ask how long it can last, who stands to gain and what happens
if the promised returns fail to arrive
Carsten Brzeski, Bert Colijn, James Knightley, Deepali Bhargava,
James Smith, Lynn Song, Jan Frederik Slijkerman and Amrita Naik
Nimbalkar
In a nutshell
The AI boom still has room to run. Investment is likely to

continue rising in 2027, supported by strong demand
and constrained supply. But its contribution to US growth
is probably close to its peak as the sheer scale of
investment makes further acceleration harder.
The boom is entering a new phase. The story is
expanding beyond chips and data centres into power
generation, grids and cooling. At the same time,
ﬁnancing is shifting gradually from hyperscalers'
enormous cash ﬂows towards debt, private credit and

### Page 2

more complex structures, increasing the risks if expected
returns fail to materialise.
The economic eﬀects are becoming broader and more
complicated. AI is supporting US growth and global
technology supply chains, but may also be diverting
investment from other parts of the economy. It is adding
to inﬂationary pressures today through demand for
energy, equipment and skilled labour, while the hoped-
for productivity and disinﬂation beneﬁts are likely to take

longer to emerge.
The winners will not necessarily be those building the
most AI infrastructure. Europe and Asia are beneﬁting
from critical positions in the supply chain, while
ultimately the largest gains may go to companies and
economies that use AI most eﬀectively.
Our base case is a plateau, not a bust. Today's leading
technology companies are highly proﬁtable and much
less leveraged than their dotcom predecessors. But
expectations are exceptionally high. The crucial question

for 2027 is whether AI revenues and productivity gains
begin to justify the extraordinary investment. If they do
not, overcapacity, tighter ﬁnancing and falling valuations
could turn a plateau into a much harder landing.
How big, how long?
Railroads, electriﬁcation, dotcom ﬁbre: how
does today's AI boom compare with the
great investment manias of the past?
Bigger than the dotcom boom, more important for growth and, so
far, more safely ﬁnanced.
Investment in US data centre structures, computers and software

reached 5.6% of GDP in the second quarter of 2026, up from 3.8% in
2022, or 5.2% once imported hardware is netted out. That already

### Page 3

puts the current boom above the dotcom peak in 2000, when
business investment in computers, communications equipment
and software reached about 4.5% of GDP, although still below
Britain’s railway mania of the 1840s, when railway investment
alone reached roughly 6-7% of GDP.
So while this is not the biggest investment boom in history, it is
already bigger than the last great technology boom. And it is
having a much bigger economic impact: IT investment accounted

for 16-18% of US GDP growth in 1998-2000; AI-related investment
now accounts for 37%.
The biggest diﬀerence though is how this boom is being ﬁnanced.
Railways and telecom networks were built on debt and ended in
defaults, largely through unrated railroad bonds in the 19th century
and high-yield telecom debt after 2000. This time, investment has
so far been funded mainly from the cash ﬂows of some of the
world’s most proﬁtable companies, with debt and equity only now

being added at the margin.
The assets are diﬀerent, too. Rails and ﬁbre lasted for decades. Data
centre shells, grids and power plants will also have long lives, but
GPUs may have an economic life of just three to six years. That
much shorter shelf life is central to the debate over whether today’s
investment will generate suﬃcient returns.
Still, the payoﬀ could come faster. Factories took decades to
redesign their ﬂoors around the electric motor. AI coding tools are

paying oﬀ within months, and adoption is already disrupting
software, accounting, online retail and advertising.
The common thread is that transformative technology does not
necessarily mean good returns for those who ﬁnance the
transformation. The Nasdaq lost almost 80% between 2000 and
2002, yet the ﬁbre laid during the boom went on to underpin the
streaming economy. Investors build the road; others get to drive on
it.
How much more will be invested in AI in

2027, and is the boom still accelerating?

### Page 4

Still accelerating in dollars, but probably peaking in its
contribution to growth.
Momentum remains strong. Taiwan's leading semiconductor
companies report order books that are full on a rolling 18-month
basis, with any new capacity snapped up as soon as it comes
online. Industry forecasts point to tight supply-demand conditions
until 2028. Barring a wave of order cancellations or defaults, that
points to another year of heavy AI investment in 2027, with

hyperscaler capex expected to exceed US$1 trillion, increasingly
geared towards AI infrastructure.
So is the boom peaking? In dollar terms, no. The constraint today is
not appetite but supply: chips, power, permits and skilled
electricians. When the bottleneck is the factory gate rather than the
order book, spending is more likely to be stretched out than cut
back.
But economists should be careful what they wish for. GDP growth
responds to the change in investment, not its level. Investment that

rises 50% a year adds a lot to growth; investment that rises 10%
from a much bigger base adds far less; and investment that merely
holds steady at a record high adds nothing at all. As the base gets
bigger, the arithmetic gets tougher. That is why we expect AI
investment to keep rising in 2027 while its contribution to US
growth should peak.
What would signal a genuine top? Shortening lead times for chips
and equipment, cancelled or postponed data centre projects, capex

guidance that stops being revised up, and a widening gap between
investment and AI revenues.
For now, the bigger risk is not that the money runs out in 2027, but
that it keeps ﬂowing so fast that the questions about returns grow
much louder in 2028.
Chips and data centres are only the ﬁrst
layer. How big is the second wave of

### Page 5

investment in grids, power generation and
cooling?
Bigger, slower and longer-lived than the ﬁrst wave – and
potentially more important for the macro outlook.
As a rule of thumb, US$1bn buys roughly 25MW of fully equipped
AI compute capacity. Not all of the c.US$1 trillion of hyperscaler
capex expected in 2027 will go to AI compute; some will fund
conventional cloud infrastructure. But even if only half does, that
implies more than 12GW of additional power demand – roughly the

output of a dozen large nuclear reactors, needed around the clock.
That is where the second wave comes in: transmission lines,
substations and transformers, gas turbines and renewables,
battery storage and industrial cooling. In the US, grid connection
queues and transformer lead times have already become binding
constraints, and some developers are building their own generation
on site rather than waiting for the grid.
This second wave diﬀers from the ﬁrst in three ways. First, the

assets last for decades rather than years. A GPU may be obsolete
by 2030; a substation built today will still be humming in 2060.
That makes grid investment less vulnerable to a sudden AI
downturn and more like the railway build-out of the 19th century.
Second, it is slower. Permitting, planning and construction take
years, which spreads the investment impulse over time and could
stretch out the cycle. Third, it is more widely shared: grid and power

equipment draws on suppliers in Europe and Asia as well as US
construction and engineering ﬁrms, so the beneﬁts travel further.
There is a catch. Somebody has to pay for the grid, and in
regulated markets that somebody is often the household electricity
customer. Rising power bills are already turning data centres into a
political issue in parts of the US, and they feed straight into the
inﬂation debate.
Chips are the sprint. Power is the marathon – and the marathon

may end up mattering more.

### Page 6

Is this an American party, or does Europe
get an invitation? Who supplies the picks
and shovels?
So far it is very much an American party – but Europe supplies the
catering, and may turn out to be the smarter guest.
The investment gap is stark. US imports of computer equipment
and semiconductors, a rough proxy for AI investment, have risen by
about 258% since 2019, compared with 38% in the UK. Europe's
own build-out is held back by high energy prices, scarce grid

capacity – think of the data centre moratoria in Dublin and
Amsterdam – and fragmented capital markets. The EU's AI
gigafactories initiative is an attempt to catch up.
But Europe sells crucial picks and shovels. ASML expects €44-45bn
of sales in 2026 and says customers keep raising their equipment-
demand forecasts because of the scale and duration of AI
investment. Inﬁneon expects its AI-related revenues to rise from
around €1.5bn in FY2026 to €2.5bn in FY2027. Add electrical and

grid equipment, cables, power generation and cooling, and
Europe's industrial base is well plugged into the American build-
out.
Asia has been one of the biggest beneﬁciaries of the AI boom. The
gains extend well beyond Korea and China, which have beneﬁted
from surging demand for memory chips and advanced technology
hardware. Across ASEAN, economies have also attracted signiﬁcant
FDI into semiconductors, electronics manufacturing, and
supporting supply chains as ﬁrms expand capacity and diversify

production networks.
More importantly, the broad economic gains from AI come from
using it, not building it – and here Europe is surprisingly good.
Uptake is strong, and European SMEs use AI almost as intensively
as large corporates, because the upfront investment is low. Nor
does AI look like a winner-takes-all technology: switching costs are
low and network eﬀects limited. If model providers end up
competing on price, the spoils go to the users – and Europe does

### Page 7

not need to build nearly as many data centres to compete. Late to
the construction site, Europe could still be early to move in.
There is a silver lining, too. Europe is far less exposed if the boom
turns to bust. Its AI capex is smaller and the wealth channel much
weaker: equities make up 13% of UK households' ﬁnancial assets,
versus 44% in the US. Missing the party also means skipping the
hangover.
Which sectors stand to win most, and which
risk being left behind?

The winners sell the infrastructure or use AI to do more; the losers
are those whose business model is the very task AI now performs.
The clearest winners are the suppliers of the build-out:
semiconductors and chip equipment, utilities and grid operators,
electrical equipment, construction, cooling and data centre real
estate. They are already seeing it in their order books.
Software is mixed. AI is a powerful selling point, but it also
threatens the per-seat pricing model: if one employee with an AI

agent does the work of ﬁve, the customer needs fewer licences.
Most at risk are legacy IT services and business process
outsourcing, call centres, parts of professional services, and media
and advertising models that depend on search traﬃc. AI-based
services may also disrupt online retail, including online travel
agencies, as AI assistants increasingly do the browsing and the
booking.
But don't write oﬀ every exposed sector. The Jevons paradox –
cheaper supply raising demand – could favour some of them. Legal

services is a good example: if AI makes contract review or legal
advice much cheaper, demand may rise faster than the cost per
task falls. Growth may also come from unexpected places. In
healthcare, AI-driven drug discovery and diagnostics could open up
new treatments, creating demand rather than merely cutting costs.
Ultimately, productivity gains depend more on the tasks
employees perform than on the sector they sit in. Workers handling

In short, the winners will be those that build AI or use it to work
smarter. The losers will be those whose core activities can
increasingly be automated.

Growth, prices and trade
How much of US growth in 2027 will come
from AI capex, and what would growth look
like without it?
Today, more than a third. In 2027, probably less – which is exactly
why the rest of the economy matters.
At ﬁrst glance, AI looks like the engine of the entire US economy.
Data centre construction, equipment investment and software
spending have grown at a pace that makes their headline
contribution to GDP growth look spectacular. But much of that

money leaves the country straight away: from Taiwanese chips to
servers assembled abroad, the hardware is largely imported.
Netting out those imports, we estimate that AI-related investment
accounts for 37% of US real GDP growth. Substantial, but a long
way from the 'AI is the only game in town' narrative.
The more uncomfortable ﬁnding lies outside tech. Rather than
lifting the overall growth rate, the AI boom seems to have
cannibalised growth elsewhere. Strip out AI, and the rest of US

private non-residential investment had fallen year-on-year for six
consecutive quarters by mid-2026. Before 2021, tech and non-tech
investment moved broadly in step; since then, they have moved in
opposite directions. One explanation: shareholders reward
companies that embrace AI, so capital budgets are being redirected
towards tech at the expense of everything else. So the boom isn't

### Page 9

so much about creating additional investment as redirecting
investment from elsewhere in the economy.
What would US growth look like without AI? Not simply 37% lower.
Part of the capital, engineers and electricity absorbed by AI would
have found other uses. A world without the AI boom would most
likely show weaker growth, but not by the full amount of AI's
contribution – and with a more balanced investment mix.
For 2027, our plateau base case implies a smaller AI contribution to

growth. That would give the non-tech economy room to breathe –
but only if it is ready to take over.
For now, AI is a capex story, not a consumer story. And capex
stories have a habit of ending with a hangover.
The productivity question: when, if ever,
does AI show up in the statistics?
In the US and China, it already does – partly. The big gains are still
to come, and may arrive abruptly.
It depends on where you look. In the US, labour productivity has

picked up markedly since the start of the AI boom. Part of that
comes from high-skilled services, exactly where you would expect
AI to show up ﬁrst. But part of it reﬂects the enormous data centre
investments, which are capital-heavy and need very little labour.
Total factor productivity – the best available measure of gains from
technological progress – has improved decently in the US, but
without an exceptional acceleration. While AI-related sectors are

providing a signiﬁcant boost to several Asian economies, this
largely reﬂects rapid investment and higher capital utilisation
rather than a sustained improvement in underlying productivity.
However, in China, we’ve seen labour productivity growth pick up in
the last two years, breaking what had been an extended downtrend
as Chinese companies race to incorporate AI in workﬂows. In the
eurozone, which lacks comparable AI investment, labour
productivity growth remains lacklustre, with very few exceptions,

like for example, in the Netherlands.

### Page 10

The technology holds real promise for broad-based productivity
gains. But reaping them requires wider adoption and investment in
infrastructure and skills, both within companies and across the
economy. That suggests a J-curve: heavy upfront intangible
investment – time spent learning new skills and reorganising
workﬂows – could initially mute measured productivity before the
investment pays oﬀ, possibly quite abruptly. Electriﬁcation is the
classic precedent: the electric motor arrived in the 1880s, but

factory productivity only took oﬀ in the 1920s, once factories had
been rebuilt around it.
And even then, a shift of the workforce towards sectors where
productivity gains are harder to achieve – think healthcare and care
work – would dampen the macroeconomic eﬀect. Measurement
doesn't help either: when AI makes a free digital service better, GDP
statistics barely notice.
Still, after decades of slowing productivity growth, AI is likely to be a
game changer. The question is not so much whether it will show up

in the statistics, but when. In 1987, Robert Solow quipped that you
could see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity
statistics. It took about a decade to prove him wrong.
Is AI inﬂationary or deﬂationary?
Electricity, memory chips and construction
costs point one way; long-run efﬁciency
gains point the other.
Inﬂationary today, disinﬂationary tomorrow – but don't hold your
breath for the outright deﬂation Silicon Valley promises.
It depends on your time horizon. For now, the eﬀect runs in the

inﬂationary direction. AI's enormous energy demand is adding to
the current energy shock, memory chip prices have surged, and the
scramble for electricians, transformers and construction capacity is
pushing up costs. Meanwhile, the investment boom is pouring fuel
on the ﬁre of a US economy already running with above-target
inﬂation.

### Page 11

In the longer run, once the technology lifts productivity, it should
lower unit labour costs and exert disinﬂationary pressure across the
economy. Like most technological breakthroughs, AI will probably
end up being disinﬂationary. But the eﬀect will be concentrated in
services and much smaller for goods with physical inputs.
Algorithms can't make steel, copper or concrete cheaper.
There is also a less comfortable twist. AI allows companies to price

dynamically, adjusting prices to what each customer is willing to
pay and thereby capturing more of the consumer surplus. That
could make some prices stickier rather than lower. Together with
the physical constraints, this makes the outright deﬂation Sam
Altman envisages rather unlikely.
For AI itself, the picture is diﬀerent. As we argued in 2024, we expect
a downward price trend for AI services, as with other software
categories, and at the sector level we continue to hold that view.

Competition between model providers should keep the pressure on.
For central banks, this means the AI disinﬂation dividend is a story
for the second half of the decade, not for 2027. Until then, the
boom adds to demand faster than it adds to supply – the textbook
recipe for inﬂation, not deﬂation.
How is AI reshaping global trade and
investment ﬂows, from Taiwanese chips to
Dutch lithography?
AI is redrawing global trade around a single supply chain – and

rewarding the countries that own its choke points.
AI is increasingly sorting the global economy into winners and
laggards. The IMF has raised its outlook for economies beneﬁting
from the AI investment boom, particularly those embedded in the
global technology supply chain, while countries outside it see
much less of a boost. Taiwan is riding booming semiconductor
demand, South Korea is beneﬁting from demand for high-
bandwidth memory, and the eﬀects travel upstream to the

suppliers of advanced chipmaking equipment, including the
Netherlands.

### Page 12

This illustrates how AI is reshaping both trade and investment
ﬂows. Spending on data centres and computing capacity in the US
drives demand for chips from Asia, which in turn requires
investment in semiconductor capacity and equipment from Europe,
Japan and the US. The result is an international AI investment
chain, with growth increasingly ﬂowing to the countries that hold
its critical links. It also explains a good part of the surge in the US
import bill: every data centre built in Texas shows up in Taiwan's

export statistics.
Trade policy is the second force reshaping the chain. Export controls
on advanced chips to China, tariﬀs and subsidy programmes such
as the US Chips Act (or direct government stakes in companies) are
pushing companies to build capacity closer to home or in friendly
countries. That makes the chain more resilient on paper, but also
more expensive – another reason why the deﬂationary promise of
AI may take longer to materialise.
The ﬂip side is concentration risk. If the chain snaps – through a

demand slump, an escalation of export controls or a Taiwan crisis –
the countries beneﬁting most today would feel it ﬁrst and hardest.
What does AI mean for labour markets, and
for how central banks read them?
So far, AI is reshaping who gets hired rather than how many are
employed – which makes it a real-time headache for central
banks.
Evidence on AI's labour market impact remains fragmented, and
the economy-wide eﬀect is still limited. If history is any guide, fears

of mass unemployment are overdone: with every major technology
of past centuries, growth was strong enough to create jobs
elsewhere, even as demand for some types of labour fell. But if
adoption is so fast that labour demand and supply can't adjust in
time, unemployment could rise temporarily.
The ﬁrst cracks are showing at the entry level. In the US, job growth
has remained positive but weaker in sectors with high AI exposure;
evidence from the eurozone is mixed. The Dallas Fed ﬁnds that

### Page 13

employment among young workers in AI-exposed occupations has
weakened, mainly reﬂecting lower hiring rather than more layoﬀs.
According to the Stanford Digital Economy Lab, employment
among 22-25-year-olds in highly exposed occupations was around
19% below where it would have been by June 2026 had it kept
pace with less-exposed occupations. In China, we see similar
pressures on entry-level work reﬂected in rising youth
unemployment. The Chinese government has planned training and

job replacement policies to help alleviate any negative eﬀects of AI
on employment. For developing Asia, particularly India and the
Philippines where outsourcing sectors are highly exposed to AI, the
evidence points to adaptation rather than decline. AI is changing
the nature of outsourced work, not reducing demand for it.
AI is not ﬁring people; it is quietly not hiring them. Still, none of this
points to mass displacement. Yet. And with ageing populations and

persistent labour shortages, the real question may be whether
shortages or AI turn out to be the bigger problem.
For central banks, AI is less of a short-run issue than you might
expect. Higher productivity growth combined with more muted job
growth could be the healthy sign of an economy absorbing a new
technology, and come with more moderate inﬂation. The tricky
part is reading it in real time. A soft payrolls print could signal either
a weakening economy or a more productive one – and the right

policy response would be very diﬀerent. Get it wrong, and a central
bank either cuts rates into a productivity boom or sits on its hands
as the economy weakens.
Who pays?
Is the boom still funded from cash ﬂows, or
are hyperscalers increasingly turning to
debt?
Still mostly from cash ﬂows – but the cushion is thinning and the
borrowing has begun.

### Page 14

The largest hyperscalers have so far paid for the AI build-out out of
their own pockets, which is the biggest diﬀerence from the dotcom
and railway booms. But capex is absorbing a rising share of
operating cash ﬂow, squeezing free cash ﬂow and leaving less room
for share buybacks. For companies that used to return cash to
shareholders at a record pace, that is a notable shift in priorities.
Borrowing is stepping up. Several hyperscalers, such as Amazon,

Alphabet, Meta, and Oracle have issued jumbo bond deals since
2025, with Oracle the most leveraged of the group. Equity markets
are being tapped too: several big tech ﬁrms announced plans to
raise equity in June. None of this is alarming on its own – these are
investment-grade companies with strong balance sheets – but it
marks a change of phase. Cash-funded booms are self-limiting:
they stop when the cash runs out. Debt-funded booms can run
further, and fall harder.

The key is to distinguish between the players. The cash-rich
hyperscalers can absorb a disappointment in AI returns; their core
businesses in advertising, cloud and software keep generating cash
regardless. The neoclouds and AI labs are a diﬀerent story. They
depend entirely on outside money – venture capital, private credit,
vendor ﬁnancing and strategic investors – and their business
models only work if revenue keeps growing spectacularly. They are

the most likely point of stress if ﬁnancing conditions tighten.
In between sits a growing grey zone of leases, joint ventures and
special purpose vehicles that keep debt oﬀ the hyperscalers' own
books.
The direction of travel is clear. The boom started as a self-funded
venture by the richest companies in the world. It is gradually
turning into a credit story.
Leases, SPVs, private credit: how opaque
has AI ﬁnancing become?
More opaque than the hyperscalers' balance sheets suggest – but

so far, the risk rests on some of the strongest borrowers in the
world.

### Page 15

A growing part of AI ﬁnancing is moving oﬀ balance sheet. Special
purpose vehicles and joint ventures, such as Meta's Hyperion data
centre ﬁnanced with Blue Owl, long-term leases and GPU-backed
loans allow companies to fund the build-out without the debt
appearing directly on their own books. Securitisation of data centre
assets through asset-backed securities and commercial mortgage-
backed securities is growing as well, bringing a wider pool of

investors into the game.
Leases are a particular blind spot. They are recognised as on-
balance-sheet commitments only once contracts are ﬁnal; until
then, the extent of incremental lease liabilities is uncertain. There is
also the question of who carries the risk if the tenant walks away
after the initial term. Although initial lease terms of 15 years are
not uncommon, these can still be shorter than the economic life of
the underlying infrastructure, leaving some residual risk around

renewal or re-leasing.
That said, risk has to be assessed contract by contract. The largest
technology ﬁrms, with very solid balance sheets, are well placed to
take on additional liabilities, and much of the oﬀ-balance-sheet
structure ultimately rests on their creditworthiness. The problem is
less hidden insolvency than hidden concentration: it is hard to see
how much of the system leans on the same handful of tenants.
Opacity also complicates the macro picture. When ﬁnancing is

spread across SPVs, private credit funds and securitisations, it
becomes harder for investors, regulators and central banks to see
where risks are concentrated – and harder to judge how a shock
would travel.
Looking ahead, capital may eventually become a major constraint
on the AI build-out, alongside permitting and access to power. The
more the industry relies on creative ﬁnancing, the more it depends
on investors' continued willingness to lend. Financial engineering

can prolong a boom, but it can't make the returns appear.
How exposed are banks, and is private
credit the shadow banking of this cycle?

### Page 16

Direct bank exposure looks modest; private credit is the obvious
weak spot – but contagion would run through markets rather than
bank failures.
Banks' direct exposure to AI looks limited. The hyperscalers fund
themselves mainly through cash ﬂow and bond markets rather
than bank loans. Indirect exposure is harder to gauge: banks lend to
private credit funds, data centre developers and construction ﬁrms.
Private credit is the obvious weak spot. Is it the shadow banking of

this cycle? Partly. It has less of the maturity mismatch that made
pre-2008 shadow banking so fragile: much of the capital is locked
up in closed-end funds, so investors can't run. But it shares other
features: opacity, infrequent valuation marks and leverage on
leverage, with funds borrowing from banks to lend to borrowers
that are themselves highly leveraged. And it is increasingly exposed
to AI through loans to neoclouds, GPU-backed ﬁnancing and data

centre developers.
The pressure point is the semi-liquid vehicles sold to retail and
wealth clients. They promise periodic liquidity on assets that are
anything but liquid. In a downturn, redemption requests could force
managers to impose gates, damaging conﬁdence across the asset
class. So far, these vehicles are not core to the AI build-out, but they
are where a shock could turn into a story.
If stress does materialise, we would expect contagion to run

through tighter ﬁnancial conditions and wider credit spreads across
the economy rather than through bank failures. That is less
dramatic than 2008, but not harmless: a repricing of credit risk
would raise funding costs far beyond tech, hitting the very non-tech
investment that is already struggling.
Banks may well escape an AI bust largely unscathed. The economy
they lend to may not.
Vendor ﬁnancing déjà vu: should we worry
when chipmakers fund their own

customers?

### Page 17

Worth watching, but this is not Lucent and Nortel all over again –
mainly because the balance sheets are much stronger.
The dotcom precedent is uncomfortable. Lucent, Nortel and Cisco
lent money to telecom start-ups so they could buy their
equipment. When the customers went bust, the suppliers took the
hit, and much of the reported demand turned out to have been
ﬁnanced by the sellers themselves.
Today, there are echoes. Chipmakers are taking stakes in AI labs

and neoclouds that then buy their chips. The same handful of ﬁrms
raise money, buy chips, lease compute and book revenue oﬀ one
another. The concern is less solvency than signal: circular deals
inﬂate reported demand and blur true end-demand. Revenue that
only exists because the supplier paid for it isn't demand.
But the picture diﬀers in important ways. Many of these contracts
appear to be normal commercial arrangements, and AI labs also
generate fast-growing revenues from customers outside these

related deals. AI-related revenues are still only part of the much
larger businesses of the hyperscalers. And the macro backdrop is
very diﬀerent: the overall US corporate debt-to-GDP ratio is not
worrisome, in stark contrast to the leverage that built up before the
dotcom bust and the global ﬁnancial crisis. Circularity in a cash-rich
system is a transparency problem; circularity in a leveraged system
is a solvency problem.
That does not mean investors can relax. Circular deals create

concentration risk: if one key AI lab stumbles, the shock would
travel quickly through its suppliers, landlords and investors, who are
often the same companies. And they make it harder to judge how
much genuine end-demand there is for AI – precisely the question
on which the whole boom rests.
The test is simple: follow the money until it reaches a customer
who isn't also an investor. The longer that trail gets, the more
nervous we should become.
Higher Fed rates helped end the dotcom

bubble. Could monetary policy or bond

### Page 18

yields pop this one too?
Probably not as the trigger – but monetary policy could make any
bust more painful, because the safety net is thinner than in 2001.
In 1999-2000, the Fed hiked rates to 6.5% before the dotcom
bubble burst, then cut by almost ﬁve percentage points in 2001.
This time, an energy shock is keeping inﬂation above target. Actual
rate hikes and more rate hikes to come could bring both the Federal
Reserve and European Central Bank closer to risky territory.

High policy rates do not necessarily stop AI investment ﬁrst. If
expected returns on AI remain very high, capital may keep ﬂowing
in while ﬁnancing becomes uneconomic for lower-return
investment elsewhere. Tight money could deepen the split between
tech and non-tech investment rather than end the boom.
At the moment, we expect hyperscaler funding needs to increase
further as investment in AI infrastructure, data centres and power
capacity accelerates. We forecast total issuance of around

US$280bn in 2027, including approximately US$190bn in USD and
US$65bn in EUR (€60bn). For European credit markets, we estimate
a net TMT supply number of €103bn, which includes US$65bn
coming from hyperscalers.
The long end of the yield curve matters more than the policy rate.
As the AI build-out becomes increasingly debt-funded, higher bond
yields and term premia raise the cost of ﬁnancing long-term
investments, from data centres to power plants. A sustained rise in

long-term yields would therefore hit hardest where the build-out
depends most on external funding.
The bigger issue is the safety net. If the bubble bursts, central banks
can't come to the rescue as aggressively as in 2001 while inﬂation
is above target. Fiscal space is also far more limited than a quarter
of a century ago.
But don't rule out a diﬀerent kind of rescue. AI is not just an
economic tool; it is also about power – geopolitical and
technological. If things went south for the sector, some form of

government support would be conceivable, from public

### Page 19

procurement to guarantees or targeted support for strategically
important companies. The dotcom bust was left to the market. An
AI bust might not be.
Monetary policy is unlikely to pop this bubble. But it could decide
how hard the landing is – and Washington may decide who gets a
parachute.
Bubble or not?
What do valuations and revenue gaps tell
us?
Bubble-like pockets, but not a bubble across the board: the leaders
are hugely proﬁtable, while the real uncertainty sits with the AI

labs and the revenue gap.
Valuations ﬁrst. At its 2000 peak, Cisco traded at well over 100
times earnings. Today’s AI leaders are expensive, but proﬁtable,
and analysts expect earnings growth to bring their multiples down
considerably. Microsoft’s P/E is expected to fall from 29.2x based on
ﬁscal 2026 earnings to 21.9x in 2028, Amazon’s from 28x based on
2025 earnings to 21x in 2027, and Alphabet’s from 20.9x to 17.9x
over the same period. With earnings estimates being higher than a

couple of months ago, that does not look particularly stretched.
The larger uncertainty sits with the AI labs, whose valuations follow
the venture capital playbook: spectacular revenue growth,
uncertain long-term economics.
Then the revenue gap. Capex is running ahead of AI revenues, as it
can take several years for investment to translate fully into
revenues. Amazon, Microsoft and Alphabet have invested around
US$600bn over 2023-25. As a rough rule of thumb, every dollar of

AI infrastructure may need to generate 40-50 cents of annual
revenue (assuming hyperscaler-like margins), implying US$240-
300bn a year – although not all of this capex was AI-related. That
looks large, but less so next to labour costs. At US$100,000 per
employee, US$300bn is the cost of three million workers. AI doesn't

### Page 20

need to replace them: a 20% productivity gain across 15 million
employees would deliver the same value. Consumers alone won't
close the gap; the money has to come from enterprises.
Two caveats. Concentration: the weight of the largest tech stocks in
the S&P 500 exceeds the dotcom peak, so any disappointment
would hit the whole index hard. And circularity in ﬁnancing and
revenues makes true end-demand harder to measure.
Finally, a bubble in prices doesn't mean the technology is a dud.

Railways had a bubble. So did the internet. Both still changed the
world.
What could end the boom prematurely:
disappointing demand, power bottlenecks, a
cheaper-model breakthrough, or Taiwan?
Most likely not a single trigger, but a slow squeeze: overcapacity,
eroding margins and tighter ﬁnancing if the revenue gap fails to
close.
Start with the usual suspects. Demand could disappoint if
enterprise adoption stalls and pilots fail to turn into proﬁts. Power

bottlenecks are more likely to cap the pace of the boom than to end
it, and could even stretch it out. A Taiwan crisis remains a low-
probability, extreme-impact event.
Cheaper models are the most intriguing risk. The DeepSeek shock of
January 2025 wiped almost US$600bn oﬀ Nvidia's market value in
a single day. Cheaper compute can raise demand – the Jevons
paradox – but higher volumes can come with lower margins. That
makes the proﬁtability of AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic the

big question mark. If it disappoints, the hardware will still be
needed, but the build-out would likely slow. Nvidia's exceptionally
high margins could also normalise if competition increases or the
scarcity value of leading-edge compute declines.
Missing from most lists is ﬁnancing: rising bond yields, a credit
event at a leveraged neocloud or private credit fund, or an energy
shock that keeps central banks hawkish.

### Page 21

Ultimately, we see the biggest risk in overcapacity. Continued
innovation in chips, more eﬃcient models and optimised
algorithms could eventually create an imbalance between supply
and demand, pushing down utilisation and returns. For now,
though, the industry is still struggling to scale compute and secure
enough power, while new business models and applications keep
emerging.
There is one more way to read today's capacity commitments: as
strategic entry deterrence. Announcing gigantic investment plans

discourages competitors from entering the market – and some of
those plans may never be fully executed if returns disappoint. That
would be a quieter ending than a crash. Not with a bang, but with a
series of downward revisions to capex guidance.
If tech stocks fall 30%, how much does the
wealth effect hit US consumption?
Less than you might think, but more than during the dotcom bust –
and the real damage would come through investment and jobs.
US equity wealth is highly concentrated. The top 20% of

households by income (earning more than $155k a year) hold
84.1% of equities, mutual funds and deﬁned-contribution pension
assets. That limits the wealth eﬀect. Fed research suggests the
marginal propensity to consume out of wealth fell from around 3.5
cents per dollar (1964-2011) to 2.6 cents in 2020.
A 30% stock market correction would wipe out roughly $24.5tn of
household wealth. At 2.6 cents per dollar, that means a drop in
consumer spending of around $640bn, or 2.9% of nominal

consumer spending (an annualised $22.3tn in August). With wealth
even more concentrated than in 2020, the multiplier is probably
weaker; at 2.2 cents, the hit would be around 2.4%. That is a level
eﬀect, typically playing out over one to two years – but a big
number nonetheless.
History oﬀers some comfort: during the dotcom bust, US consumer
spending barely ﬂinched. But it could be worse this time. First,
wealthy households are currently the only ones holding up

### Page 22

spending; the bottom 60%, who spend roughly as many dollars as
the top 20%, are squeezed by tariﬀs and higher fuel prices. Second,
the Fed has less room to cut than in 2001. Third, the credit cushion
is thinner: rising delinquencies on car loans and credit cards show
stress building at lower incomes.
The good news: this is not 2008. US household debt stands at
around 70% of GDP, the same as in 2001 and well below the 100%
reached in 2007.
In the end, the wealth eﬀect is the smaller channel. After the

dotcom bust, business investment fell by 11% in two years. IT
equipment was the least of it, falling just 6%, transport equipment
fell 21% and manufacturing buildings plunged by a whopping
55%. Tech may light the fuse, but the explosion happens elsewhere.
What would a tech sell-off mean for the
dollar and FX markets?
Not necessarily the classic safe-haven dollar rally – this time, a
tech sell-oﬀ could well hurt the dollar.
The usual playbook says risk-oﬀ means dollar up. But an AI-led

correction would be a US-centric shock. Foreign investors hold large,
partly unhedged positions in US equities, built up over a decade of
American outperformance. A sell-oﬀ in US tech could trigger
repatriation and higher hedge ratios, both dollar-negative – as in
the 'sell America' episode of April 2025. The IMF estimates the
dollar would fall by around 6% in an AI-led equity correction.
The rates channel points in the same direction. Negative wealth

eﬀects, weaker consumption and slower US growth would
eventually push the Fed towards rate cuts, narrowing interest rate
diﬀerentials against the dollar. The timing would depend on
inﬂation: with prices still running above target, the Fed may cut
later than markets hope, which could limit the dollar's fall in the
early stages.
Among the winners, the yen and the Swiss franc would play their
traditional safe-haven role. The euro could beneﬁt relatively, given

Europe's lower exposure to both AI capex and equity wealth (4Q). A

tech crash made in America would, for once, be a reason to hold
European assets rather than ﬂee them.
The losers would be the currencies of Asian tech exporters, notably
the Taiwan dollar and the Korean won, whose economies are
leveraged bets on AI demand. They would come under heavy
pressure, although intervention by local authorities and the legacy
of huge trade surpluses would cushion the blow somewhat.
The bottom line: in an AI bust, the dollar may lose its reputation as

the place to hide. Not because the world has found a better safe
haven, but because this time the ﬁre would start in the safe house.
Our base case for 2027: boom, plateau or
bust, and which signposts should readers
watch?
Our base case is a plateau at altitude: the boom continues in 2027,
but its contribution to growth peaks. A bust remains the main risk
scenario.
Early indicators point to tight semiconductor supply into 2028,
suggesting the investment cycle still has legs. But as capex growth

slows, AI's contribution to US growth should fade.
The diﬀerence between the two scenarios matters enormously for
the US. A plateau would give the non-tech economy room to
breathe: capital, energy and workers no longer absorbed by AI
could ﬂow back, allowing non-tech investment – and potentially
employment – to rebound. A bust would be a very diﬀerent animal:
an abrupt choking oﬀ of tech investment, similar to 2001,
combined with a negative wealth eﬀect on consumers through

falling stock markets. And this time, central banks would have less
room for rate cuts as inﬂation remains their main concern.
Which signposts should readers watch? For the boom itself, the key
indicators are hyperscaler capex guidance, Taiwanese order books
and lead times, and AI revenues relative to capex. Markets will focus
on realised AI revenues at the large technology ﬁrms, margins on
incremental revenue and how the next phase of the build-out will

recovers, how hiring in AI-exposed sectors develops, and what
productivity, grid connection and power data are telling us.
The question for 2027 isn't whether AI will change the world. It's
whether anyone gets paid for it in time. If earnings fail to match
sky-high expectations, the plateau could turn out to be a ledge.

Document 359: FX Daily_ Tech collapses, FX shrugs

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ Tech collapses, FX shrugs.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-06
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

A delayed reaction to the Democrat blue wave has seen some
powerful selling of tech stocks. A little surprisingly, the reaction
in FX markets has remained very muted. FX participants so far
see this as an idiosyncratic re-pricing of the tech sector, rather
than a harbinger of recession. Expect defensive FX to
outperform, rather than a broad dollar decline

Chris Turner, Frantisek Taborsky and Francesco Pesole
The Israeli shekel is particularly sensitive to swings in US tech stocks
USD: Payrolls can provide the dollar
some support
We have all seen big falls in tech stocks on the back of the threat to
data centres. The fact that the equity sell-oﬀ spread to the
defensive sectors of consumer staples, utilities and healthcare
serves as a warning that an asset management industry
underweight in cash may be tidying up positions into year-end.
After all, most global benchmark equity indices, apart from China,
are still in the green on a year-to-date basis.

### Page 2

FX markets have reacted to the equity sell-oﬀ in a typically
defensive, but muted way. In the G10 space, the high beta
Australian and New Zealand dollars are underperforming, while the
defensive Japanese yen and Swiss franc are in demand. However,
the moves have been small. So far, it seems FX participants are
prepared to see the tech sell-oﬀ as an idiosyncratic story on the
back of the Democrat blue wave, rather than a major macro
setback. Notably, the Philadelphia semiconductor index trades on a

61% one-month implied volatility compared to just 7% for USD/JPY.
This may help investors to see FX markets as a less correlated asset
class compared to the wild swings in tech.
Where we have seen the highest correlations in FX are places like
the Israeli shekel. Local investors run high FX hedge ratios on their
US investments and will now be in the process of buying back dollar
hedges on their shrinking US tech portfolios.
The big question for the dollar is whether this tech sell-oﬀ leads to a

broader re-assessment of the US growth and inﬂation trajectory. US
short-dated rates have fallen close to 50bp since late September
and the dollar's yield advantage over other major pairs has
softened a little. Feeding into that story today will be the October
US jobs report. Second-tier US job indices this week have all come in
on the strong side and there seems to be no reason to expect a soft
jobs ﬁgure today. The whisper number currently stands at +82k

versus last month's soft reading of +29k.
A decent jobs number today will conﬁrm the Fed's understanding
that the US jobs market is 'solid' and could provide some support to
short-dated rates and the dollar. We still think it is too soon to call a
broad-based dollar decline and that defensive positions in FX
markets will be expressed through cross rates like a lower AUD/JPY
or in the emerging world with a weaker South African rand or
Mexican peso (see below).

The DXY dollar index is being remarkably resilient and can continue
to trade towards the middle of a 101.50-102.50 range. Clearly, the
dollar's liquidity advantages and near 4% one-week deposit rates
are helping here.

Chris Turner
EUR: Unimpressive
It has been a rather unimpressive performance from the euro. We
know that the European buy-side is running low hedge ratios on its
US investments. Yet a large US tech sell-oﬀ is still not enough to
prompt much EUR/USD buying at all. It seems that global investors
are more than happy to leave their cash in dollars for the time
being. In other words, we are seeing a rotation into cash and not
out of the dollar. Helping that story is the return to more familiar

correlations, where US Treasuries are now performing their safe
haven role.
Barring a soft US jobs report today, expect EUR/USD to consolidate
not far from 1.12. On the eurozone calendar today is ECB Chief
Economist Philip Lane at 10:30 CET. The market still prices 18bp of
ECB hikes in December and that should remain sticky unless we see
a much deeper equity correction.
A hawkish Bank of England can see sterling continue to outperform.
The prospect of UK inﬂation heading up towards 5% in the second

quarter next year makes BoE hawkish pricing quite resilient.
EUR/GBP has overshot rate diﬀerentials down at these low 0.83
levels, but there seems to be no reason to expect an upside
correction just yet.
Chris Turner
CEE: NBP and CNB have swapped roles
The main takeaway from the Czech National Bank meeting
yesterday was the 4-3 split within the board, following a long
period of a strong majority. The market interprets this as a dovish
signal, suggesting the CNB is unwilling to hike rates further unless

the inﬂation situation deteriorates signiﬁcantly. For the ﬁrst time
since the onset of the US-Iran conﬂict, the CNB’s forecast aligns
closely with market pricing, with the PRIBOR rate peaking at around
4.55%. However, inﬂation is expected to worsen over the remainder
of the year, approaching 3% and nearing 4% in 27Q1. We do not
believe the current shift in energy prices justiﬁes such a rally in CZK

► 特斯拉3Q26財報好壞參半 — 毛利率與交付
量超預 期 ， 營收 大 致符合 ，營業 利潤 遜預
期，最大意外是自由現金流達 15億美元，高
於共識負43億，資本開支指引下調成憂慮。
► 早前公司展示下代 Roadster能力，本質上是
光環車 ，定價高受眾少 ，2028 年才有望投
產，盈利貢獻有限 。Model 3/Y成為大眾商
品，競爭激烈 ，利潤率壓縮 。正如傳統品牌
以賽道或參賽展示技術 ，「飛天」旨在展示
獨有跨界整合能力，維持品牌形象溢價。
► 至於和SpaceX的潛在合併，核心在於貫通多
頂 業 務， 提 高 資 本 運 用 效 率， 共同分擔
Terafab 的開支等 ，現時兩家合計市值約為
3.5兆美元。這項巨型潛在交易 ，除了要跨過
極高表決門檻外，SpaceX具極敏感的美國國
防及NASA合作背景 ，而特斯拉有超過 20%
業務倚重上海超級工廠 ，合併存在嚴重地緣
衝突。預期馬斯克將在川普離任前 ，加速執
行Tesla與SpaceX 合併。

► 今年9月適逢地緣擾動持續推升高油價、聯準
會重啟 升息 、財政部 與企 業發債 浪潮 等 影
響，形塑債市今年以來單月最差表現 。檢視
2021年以來美國投資級債前十大單月最差總
報酬，2022年聯準會 3月啟動升息年度就佔
一半以上，顯示利率風險仍為近5年來債市最
大風險 ，然檢視單月最差報酬表現未來期間
總報酬，除2022年1月和3月聯準會重啟升息
前夕與當月表現明顯較弱，多數於未來1年後
多呈現正報酬 ，反映隨利率走升放大孳息效
益，同時挹注債券長期持有之報酬空間。
► 今年來債券市場利率波動明顯推升信用債利
差，其 中僅 以低 信 評債利 差升幅 較顯 ，此
外，9月以來利率連動度高及AI相關產業債種
也成為利差擴大重災區 ，凸顯債市投資人減
輕市場不確定性的投資傾向 ，考量信用債市
歷經利率上行利空洗禮 ，信用利差同步擴大

創造收益空間 ，可把握債信體質仍佳企業債
鎖利良機，並透過時間複利力量強化收益。
利率波動升，僅低信評利差擴大程度相對明顯9月為美國投資級債今年最差單月報酬
用時間換空間，單月最差表現形塑未來報酬機會

► 受聯準會重啟升息影響 ，過去一年美國公債
殖利率曲線持續陡峭化 ，其中利率最大升幅
落在短天期的 2至3年期間，美國核心通膨與
就業仍具韌性 ，聯準會將持續保持 緊縮貨幣
政策步調來應對高通膨 ，考量美國高物價與
高利率環境短期 難消，宜透過縮短債券配置
天期並進行動態調整 ，為因應後續利率不確
定性環境的良策。
► 為因應利率不確定性 ，投資人可採取債券梯
的投資配置 ，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行 組合，債券梯投資重點主要在
於，根據殖利率曲線型態及利率動態建立不
同期間 債券梯，假設市場利率走升且殖利率
曲線正斜率 ，建立短天期債券梯除了降低利
率風險 ，有 短期 閒 置資金 及孳息 需求 投資
人，亦可取得穩定孳息 收益。若後續投資人
有短期資金需求 ，可以視情況將短期債券到
期後資金回收 ，降低只投資單一債券須持至
到期或直接變現的流動性風險。
投資焦點
美國公債殖利率曲線持續為正斜率
高利率波動持續，用策略迎戰市場擾動
資料來源：Bloomberg，凱基證券整理

建立短天期債券梯，分散風險及穩定現金流
債券梯
經過第2年第一檔 債券到期，視資金需求 及市況與利率 動
態決定後續配置

轉投資股市
短天期債券梯 ：假設買入 2檔各 3、4、5及7年到期債券 ，
聚焦 短期穩定收益
► 債券梯是一種分散投資方式 ，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行組合，可兼具：
1) 分散利率風險： 將資金分別買進不同到期年期的債券 ，以分
散利率風險。
2) 分散信用風險： 分散投資不同發行 機構債券，不會重壓 單一
債券並可分散信用違約風險。
3) 降低流動性風險： 若投資人有短期資金需求 ，可以視情況將
短期債券到期後資金回收 ，降低只投資單一債券須持至到期
或直接變現之流動性風險。

股市
 美股第三季獲利年增率預估為雙位數成長 ，市場靜待財報結果與展望 ，和對AI資本支出回收影響 。
考量地緣衝突 、殖利率偏高、評價面壓力與鄰近美國期中選舉 ，短期金融市場紛擾不止 ，可增加防
禦股增強耐震度 。長期AI、科技發展趨勢不變 ，逢股市拉回時可分批布局 ，分散配置於不同國家與
產業，降低市場整體波動，亦可以槓鈴策略(防禦+成長)穩中求利。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍工仍
有投資題材。
策略風格：可增加防禦股配置，中長期看
好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分關注
醫療保健生技產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，預估今年底前主要國家仍有升息空間 ，殖利率偏高環境 使中短天期債券提供
吸引力，逢利率走高、鎖利領息。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流與還款能力 ，聚焦風險調整後
利差具吸引力者，包含金融、通訊及公用事業等產業。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、通訊及公用事業等

匯市
 歐洲政治風險上升、歐元短期弱盤，美元高檔有撐。
 日本銀行再度升息時間不定，日圓區間震盪。
美元高檔有撐
商品
 中東地緣衝突短期難解，油價維持高位但震盪加劇。
 短期美國雙率走高 ，使金價短期承壓 ，惟美歐日財政赤字壓力與去美元化 ，使全球央行與長期投資
人買盤依舊，提供黃金中長期穩定支撐。

### Page 18

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

### Page 1

PROPRIETARY RESEARCH 
EARNINGS AGGREGATES 
STOXX 600 Earnings Outlook provides analysis and commentary on aggregate earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.

Analyst: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA                                         November 5, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com

STOXX 600 EARNINGS OUTLOOK  
EUROPEAN AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS

 Third quarter earnings are expected to increase 25.0% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, earnings 
are expected to increase 9.5%.

 Third quarter revenue is expected to increase 11.9% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, revenues are 
expected to increase 6.9%.

 212 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported earnings to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 58.0% reported results 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 257 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported revenue to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 64.2% reported revenue 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 During the week of November 9, 40 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

Through November 5, 212 companies in the STOXX 600 Index reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these, 58% reported 
earnings above analyst estimates. In a typical quarter (sin ce 2012), 54% of companies beat estimates and 39% miss 
estimates.

In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 4.2% above estimates, which is below the 5.9% long-term 
(since 2012) average surprise factor.

Through November 5, 257 companies in the STOXX 600 Index reported revenues for Q3 2026. Of these, 64.2% 
exceeded analyst expectations. In a typical quarter (since 2012), 58% of companies beat estimates and 42% miss 
estimates.

In aggregate, companies are reporting revenues that are 1.5% below estimates, which is below the 1.3% long-term 
(since 2012) average surprise factor.

Q3 2026: EARNINGS GROWTH RATES

The estimated earnings growth rate for the STOXX 600 fo r Q3 2026 is 25%. The STOXX 600 expects to see share-
weighted earnings of €164.4B in Q3 2026, compared to shar e-weighted earnings of €131.6B (based on the year-ago 
earnings of the current constituents) in Q3 2025.

Eight of the ten sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to Q3 2025. The Energy sector 
has the highest earnings growth rate for the quarter, wh ile the Real Estate has the weakest anticipated growth 
compared to Q3 2025.

The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (153 .4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €35.7B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €14.1B in Q3 2025.

The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (-69.6%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €0.9B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €3.1B in Q3 2025.
